<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of alchemical mixing. While the previous page focused on the physical processes of thickening and fixation, this page moves into the philosophical and "atomic" theory behind these combinations, drawing on the authority of Democritus and Hermes Trismegistus to explain how the smallest parts of matter unite to form the Philosopher's Stone.</meta>

# Brother Ferrari's

Thus, according to the teaching of **Democritus** <note>Democritus was an ancient Greek philosopher who first proposed that the universe is made of tiny, indivisible particles called atoms. Alchemists adopted this "atomism" to explain how elements could merge at a fundamental level.</note>, mixing is a union of mixable things that are thoroughly joined with one another. It is crucial to understand the nature of these mixable elements, for they are the primary origins of every combined substance. We cannot know either the visible or the hidden nature of a compound if we do not know how to mix or assemble the elements correctly. 

Therefore **Hermes** <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure considered the father of alchemy and the author of the Emerald Tablet.</note> says: "O sons of the wise, understand the science of the elements, which are manifest through their causes within their hidden appearance." For their hidden nature is never revealed except when they are combined; they do not reach perfection until their colors have been fully developed. 

Likewise, take note of the smallest parts—that is, the **indivisible** <term>indivisible</term> <note>original: *unzertheiliche*; this refers to the concept of the "atom." The author argues that for a perfect mixture to occur, the elements must be broken down into their smallest possible units so they can intermingle completely.</note> ones. For the smallest thing is that which cannot be divided; if it could be divided, it would not be the smallest. Thus we see that the mixing of elements occurs through these smallest, indivisible parts, and it is evident from its description that an element is the smallest part of a body. 

For an element is a simple and minuscule component of the physical form. Thus you have now explained, Holy Father <note>The "Holy Father" likely refers to the patron of the work, often identified in such treatises as a high-ranking cleric or even the Pope, to whom the alchemist dedicated his secrets.</note>, what the beginnings of this most precious thing are, and the manner of their assembly. This composition is nothing other than the birth of the **Medicine** <note>Alchemists often called the Philosopher's Stone a "Medicine" because of its perceived power to "heal" base metals by turning them into gold, or to heal the human body of all diseases.</note> or the **Philosopher's Stone**, which is otherwise called by the name of Gold.

<summary>This page explains the "atomic" theory of alchemy, arguing that elements must be broken down into their smallest indivisible parts to properly combine. It identifies this perfect mixture as the birth of the Philosopher's Stone, also known as the "Medicine" or "Gold."</summary>

# On the Effects of the Primary Principles.

Now follows the explanation of the effects of the previously mentioned principles, namely of **Mercury** and of **Sulfur**. For their proof, it should be noted that in the operation of this "Thing," several stages are to be considered. For <term>Lucas</term> <note>Lucas is a character in the *Turba Philosophorum* often identified with the ancient philosopher Chrysippus or Leucippus.</note> says in the <term>Assembly of the Philosophers</term> <note>original: *Turba Philosophorum*; one of the oldest and most influential Latin alchemical texts, written as a debate between ancient sages.</note>:

> You need nothing more than a single thing, and that same single thing is changed into another nature in every stage of our work.

Such stages, however, follow the various divisions of the mixable elements that occur during the operation. And every division (or proportion) in its stage—according to the order held by Nature in the manner and continuation of her birth—is called by the name of a metal. Thus, they have called the first stage of Nature’s operation Iron or **Mars**, the second stage Copper or **Venus**, another Lead or **Saturn**, another Tin or **Jupiter**, another Silver or the **Moon** <note>original: *Luna*</note>, another Gold or the **Sun** <note>original: *Sol*</note>. And with infinitely many other names, they have called these their metals in a metaphorical way, all to hide the knowledge.

However, these metals—inasmuch as they are generated from the same first principles, namely from a "First Matter" which is **Mercury** and **Sulfur**—are called, and are, the effects of the aforementioned principles, namely of Sulfur and Mercury. The Philosophers have treated each of these specifically, in which they

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Single Thing" (the First Matter) by explaining the intentionally confusing nomenclature used by alchemists. It clarifies that when authors name specific metals like Iron or Lead, they are often describing stages of a single process where one substance "dies" to give birth to another.</meta>

<meta>catchword: calls</meta>

They described these according to their distinct properties and in various ways, each on its own, because they possessed different compositions or modes of <term>birth</term> <note>original: *Gebehrung*; refers to the process of a substance coming into being or manifesting its form.</note> during their creation or production. 

It should be noted, however, that the <term>Philosophers</term> <note>In alchemical texts, "the Philosophers" refers to the lineage of alchemical authorities and sages throughout history.</note> sometimes call that which they termed Gold, their Iron or Lead; and they do the same with others. Conversely, they also call Iron or Lead—and other substances—Gold, and they do this for various reasons. For insofar as the dissolution or <term>corruption</term> <note>original: *corruption*; in the Aristotelian sense used here, it means the breaking down or "death" of one form so that a new one can arise.</note> of one thing is the birth or cause of another, and the effect remains within its cause, they spoke rightly in saying that Gold is Iron, and so on with the others. 

This is because the thing they call Gold—or the <term>Medicine of Gold</term> <note>Also known as the Tincture or the Philosopher’s Stone, which was believed to "cure" base metals of their imperfections to make them gold.</note>—is <term>generated</term> <note>original: *generiret*; produced or brought into being.</note> from the dissolution or corruption of other metals. For in a single operation, all the mentioned metals are born and corrupted. And from them, and out of those very same things, their Gold is generated. Thus they have rightly said that Iron—and likewise the others—is Gold, because the cause is, in a certain sense, its own effect. 

In this same consideration, other metals also take on each other's names, namely insofar as the corruption of one is the birth of another. It should also be noted that the Philosophers call all these metals **Mercury** and **Sulfur**, because they originate and are produced from them. However, the Philosophers have called the aforementioned births of metals <term>complexions</term> <note>original: *complexiones*; the specific "mixture" or temperament of qualities—hot, cold, wet, dry—that defines a substance.</note> (mixtures) [calls/named]...

It establishes that Gold, Iron, and Lead are often used as metaphors for stages in a single continuous operation involving Mercury and Sulfur.</summary>

### Chemical Treatise

[they] call them, but all are unequal, except for gold and silver. Silver itself they also called unequal in relation to gold, but in relation to the others, they called them equal; thus, they termed the <term>complexion</term> <note>original: *complexion*; the specific combination of qualities (hot, cold, moist, dry) that determines the nature of a substance.</note> of gold as equal and perfect.

And the <term>Philosophers</term> <note>A term for the lineage of alchemical authorities, such as Geber and Hermes.</note> sought none of the aforementioned compositions or <term>births</term> <note>original: *Gebehrungen*; refers to the process of a substance taking on its physical form.</note>, except for that one alone which they call equal—namely, that of gold. Regarding this, <term>Johannitius</term> <note>Likely the Latinized name of Hunayn ibn Ishaq, a 9th-century scholar whose medical and natural philosophy works were foundational in the Middle Ages.</note> says: That is an equal mixture when the body is called "healthy" due to a good <term>temperament</term> <note>original: *temperament*; the balanced state of a substance's internal properties.</note>.

And it is to be noted that he speaks with <term>moderation</term> <note>original: *moderation*; here meaning a state of being tempered or balanced.</note>, namely of the four natures: heat, cold, moisture, and dryness. For when one of these natures does not exceed the others, it is called a uniform body, because as much of one is present as the other. Likewise, he says "healthy"—that is, pure from everything that can cause <term>corruption</term> <note>original: *Verderbung*; the chemical or physical breakdown of a substance.</note>.

Therefore <term>Geber</term> <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan, a central figure in alchemy who proposed that all metals are composed of different proportions of mercury and sulfur.</note> speaks: Tin is a very pure lead, and within it is an equality of formation or <term>thickening</term> <note>original: *Inpissirung*; the process of a liquid substance becoming solid or dense.</note> of two constituent things—namely, <term>Mercury</term> and <term>Sulfur</term> <note>In alchemy, these are the "philosophical" principles of volatility/liquidity and combustibility/solidity.</note>. But it is not an equality of quantity, because in the mixture, the mercury has the upper hand.

Likewise <term>Hermes</term> <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary father of the "Hermetic" arts and alchemy.</note> says: You children of the wise, there are seven bodies, among which the first is gold—the best among them, the king and the head—which neither the earth corrupts, nor any burning things consume, nor is it [affected] by...

<sig>I v</sig>
<meta>catchword: the</meta>

<summary>This page explains the alchemical concept of "equality," defining gold as the only metal with a perfect balance of the four natures (heat, cold, moisture, dryness). It cites authorities like Johannitius, Geber, and Hermes to explain that metals like tin and lead are "unbalanced" versions of this same underlying mercury-sulfur composition.</summary>
<keywords>gold, silver, equality, complexion, temperament, four natures, Johannitius, Geber, Hermes, mercury, sulfur, tin, lead, corruption</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the balanced temperament of gold, which was established on the previous page as the perfect "equal" mixture of the four primary qualities. It transitions from the nature of the metal itself to the practical alchemical operations required to produce the "thing we seek" (the Philosopher's Stone or the Great Elixir).</meta>

# Brother Ferrari #

[by] the water is changed / because its mixture is <term>tempered</term> <note>original: *temperiret*; meaning a balanced state where no single quality dominates.</note> / and its nature is uniform in warmth / cold / dryness and moisture / and in it there is nothing too much / nor too little. And for this reason, the <term>Philosophers</term> <note>The "Philosophers" refers to the traditional lineage of alchemists and naturalists.</note> have preferred it / and held it in high regard / and said / that it is with gold among the <term>metallic bodies</term> / just as it is with the sun among the stars.

Now the remaining artificial beginnings of this thing / which we seek / must also be described / and these are its <insert>eight</insert> <note>The text says "four operations" but then lists eight; this likely refers to four primary stages or is a common manuscript discrepancy.</note> <term>operations</term> <note>original: *operationes*; the specific physical and chemical processes used in the laboratory.</note> / by means of which this thing / which we seek / is generated / and placed into a real being and brought forth. And the first type is <term>sublimation</term> <note>The process of heating a substance until it turns to vapor and then solidifies again at the top of the vessel.</note> / and the second is the descent <note>original: *Herab-steigung*; in alchemy, this is *descensio*, where substances are forced downward through heat.</note> / thirdly, <term>distillation</term> / and another of these types is <term>calcination</term> <note>Reducing a solid to a powder through high heat.</note> / and another is <term>solution</term> <note>Dissolving a solid into a liquid.</note> / and another is <term>congelation</term> <note>The cooling or thickening of a liquid into a solid.</note> / the seventh however is <term>fixation</term> <note>original: *Figirung*; making a volatile substance stable under heat.</note> / the eighth is <term>inceration</term> <note>The process of adding liquid to a substance until it reaches a wax-like consistency.</note>. Of all and each of these, the Philosophers make a special treatise / and of all and each of these they say infinite things / which types / although they are different when reckoned against each other / are yet in fact one. For the Philosophers / when they observe their <term>matter</term> / that when it is in its vessel / and feels its "sun" / that is / its warmth / it immediately steams out or <term>evaporates</term> / in the form of a very subtle smoke / and rises into the height of the vessel / then the Philosophers have called such rising a <term>sublimation</term>. But when they afterwards saw this same matter / which had risen / descend again upon the

<meta>catchword: ground</meta>

<summary>This page defines gold as the perfectly balanced metal—the "sun" of the elements—and introduces the eight essential alchemical operations used to achieve the Great Work. The author emphasizes that while these processes have different names, they are ultimately part of a single, unified transformation.</summary>
